# Review packet

156 people. For each one, check the scores against the quotes. Enter your own score in `review_sheet.csv` (`your_score`) wherever you disagree. Leave it blank if you agree.

## Bernard Sanders (Independent, VT) — *top x-risk*

Sanders frames AI as the most transformative technology in history, one that billionaire 'oligarchs' will use to replace workers and consolidate power unless the public intervenes. He increasingly stresses existential risk and loss of human control, citing scientists such as Hinton. He backs strong measures: a pause on advanced AI, a superintelligence ban, a data center moratorium, and a public stake in AI companies. He favors cooperation with China over racing it, and he also criticizes AI industry super PAC spending.

Evidence: high · 265 statements

- **ai_overall: 0.5** (runs: 1/0) — Claude: 1 (treats harms as dominant). Median of 29 labeled statements. GPT: 0 (presents AI as mainly a threat). Median of 60 labeled statements.

    - “President Trump said data centers are "money machines" and "one of the biggest driving forces in the future for jobs."

Really? Is that why Elon Musk says AI and robots "will replace all jobs"?

It might be a "money machine" for Big Tech oligarchs, but not for working families. https://t.co/Ev3wN2trzx” — x, 2026-07-16, https://x.com/SenSanders/status/2077813390638993650
    - “Yes. @RBReich is right. If the rapid advancement in AI destroys tens of millions of jobs as many Big Tech Oligarchs predict, who will be able to afford to purchase the products they are pushing?

The economic gains of AI must benefit humanity, not just a handful of billionaires.” — x, 2026-07-10, https://x.com/BernieSanders/status/2075720409605959964

## Christopher A. Coons (Democrat, DE) — *top x-risk*

Coons describes AI as having real promise but needing guardrails, and says tech companies can't be trusted to police themselves. Much of his record is bipartisan legislation on deepfakes and likeness (NO FAKES Act), election deception, and AI transparency and whistleblowers. By 2026 he frames AI as an existential-scale risk and wants it on the agenda of US–China talks, while also stressing U.S. leadership and chip export controls against China.

## Michael F. Bennet (Democrat, CO) — *top x-risk*

Bennet presents AI as a potentially transformational technology whose promise comes with serious risks, especially to children, elections and democracy. His main message, repeated across 2023–2026, is that Congress should create an independent federal digital/AI regulator, because voluntary industry commitments are not enough. He has recently cited AI insiders' warnings of catastrophic risk, and he also raises China, chip exports and competitiveness as secondary themes.

Evidence: high · 85 statements

- **ai_overall: 2.0** (runs: 2/2) — Claude: 2 (weighs benefits and risks equally). Median of 6 labeled statements. GPT: 2 (weighs benefits and risks equally). Median of 7 labeled statements.

That’s why I introduced the Digital Platform Commission Act, creating a new agency to oversee Big Tech and new tools like AI.” — x, 2023-09-16, https://x.com/SenatorBennet/status/1703077217314423085

## Marc A. Veasey (Democrat, TX) — *top x-risk*

Veasey discusses AI as both an economic and innovation opportunity and a source of risk, covering cybersecurity, privacy, civil liberties, election misinformation, and safety. He often frames it through U.S. leadership and competition with China, and through workforce and infrastructure concerns like jobs and grid strain. He supports targeted protections and criticizes the rollback of AI safeguards, without calling for sweeping regulation.

## Seth Moulton (Democrat, MA) — *top x-risk*

Moulton talks about AI mainly as a major security and societal danger, especially autonomous weapons, which he says could be as dangerous as nuclear weapons. He pushes for international rules such as a Geneva Convention for AI, strong domestic regulation, and accountability for tech companies. He also stresses beating China on AI and chips, and acknowledges AI's opportunities, while using AI literacy as a line of attack in his Senate campaign.

- **ai_overall: 1.5** (runs: 2/1) — Claude: 2 (weighs benefits and risks equally). Median of 2 labeled statements. GPT: 1 (treats harms as dominant). Median of 4 labeled statements.

He favors light-touch, transparency-focused rules and does not discuss AGI or loss of control.

## Brad Sherman (Democrat, CA) — *top x-risk*

Brad Sherman talks about AI mainly as a long-term existential and loss-of-control problem. He worries about self-aware, ambitious superintelligent systems and has introduced legislation to fund research to monitor for and prevent them. He also raises job losses, bias, creators' rights, and chip exports to China, and he favors international cooperation and regulation over a pure race with China.

## Ro Khanna (Democrat, CA) — *top x-risk*

Ro Khanna frames AI mainly through workers and economic fairness. He pushes ideas such as taxing automation, a federal jobs program, and a 'worker-first' agenda, while saying AI also has real benefits. In late 2025 and 2026 his rhetoric shifts toward catastrophic and loss-of-control risk, and he calls for a federal AI agency, pre-certification, liability, and an international safety agreement with China. He is hostile to tech billionaires writing the rules and to federal preemption of state AI laws, but he combines this with a pro-innovation, 'stay ahead of China' stance.

## Ritchie Torres (Democrat, NY) — *top x-risk*

Ritchie Torres describes AI as the most revolutionary technology of our time and says he has "measured optimism," while dismissing both apocalyptic and utopian predictions and noting signs of market over-hype. He sponsored the AI Disclosure Act and, more recently, has pressed for AI safety rules and raised existential and misalignment concerns. He frames AI strongly as a race with China, linking it to energy policy and chips, and treats superintelligence as a real prospect.

## Yassamin Ansari (Democrat, AZ) — *top x-risk*

Early in the record, Ansari focused on local and concrete AI issues: data center water and energy use, deepfakes, impersonation of federal officials, and jobs. She also opposed the federal moratorium on state AI laws. From September 2026 on, she became a leading voice on frontier AI risk. She warns of uncontrollable superintelligence and existential threats, and she calls for a pause, a ban on superintelligence, a new federal agency, and a global treaty that includes China. She rejects the framing of an AI race with China.

## Micah Lasher (Democratic, NY) — *top x-risk*

Micah Lasher frames AI as a rapidly transforming force that needs strong oversight. He warns of civilization-level risks and a possible window of gravest danger within months. He supports aggressive federal regulation without preemption, a new AI agency, a ban on superintelligence, and immediate state attorney general action, and he also worries about jobs, data centers, and Big Tech power. He acknowledges creative and research uses of AI but mostly talks about risk and accountability.

## Marsha Blackburn (Republican, TN) — *top AGI-pilled*

Blackburn frames AI as a source of innovation that needs federal guardrails, with the focus on protecting children, creators, conservatives and communities (her 'four Cs'). She presses Big Tech hard on chatbots and kids, deepfakes, copyright and perceived anti-conservative bias, and she sponsors the NO FAKES Act, KOSA and her TRUMP AMERICA AI Act. She also talks about winning the AI race against China, but she opposed a blanket state-law moratorium until federal standards exist. Existential risk rarely comes up.

## Norma J. Torres (Democrat, CA) — *top AGI-pilled*

Norma Torres speaks about AI mostly as an economic, financial-services and congressional-operations tool, and she says she is personally pro-AI. She describes AI as transformative, calls for U.S. leadership and a race with China, and in one hearing statement uses Manhattan Project and superintelligence language. Her concerns are mainly practical: deepfakes, data privacy, accuracy of AI-drafted legislation, financial-system exposure and who pays for data centers. She supports targeted, harmonized rules rather than sweeping regulation and has called AI discourse overly apocalyptic.

## Bill Foster (Democrat, IL) — *top AGI-pilled*

Bill Foster, a physicist and chip designer on the House AI Task Force, describes AI as both an opportunity and a growing danger. His early statements focus on deepfakes, digital identity and financial-sector risks. His 2026 statements turn to recursive self-improvement, rogue AI agents and containment of superhuman AI. He regularly criticizes House leadership and the Trump administration for inaction, urges Congress to pass technically feasible guardrails, and combines US-China competition framing (chip export security) with calls for talks with China on AI safeguards.

## Pete Ricketts (Republican, NE) — *top AGI-pilled*

Ricketts talks about AI mostly through competition with China. He repeatedly says the U.S. must win the 'AI race' and backs export controls on chips and chipmaking equipment, including his MATCH Act. ## Tammy Baldwin (Democrat, WI) — *flagged: runs disagreed*

Baldwin describes AI as having real promise in research, health, weather forecasting and productivity, but she stresses accountability, worker protection and public control over Big Tech. She raises concrete harms such as algorithmic care decisions, deepfakes and scams, chatbot-induced harm, job displacement and data-center costs. In her most recent statements she backs strong oversight of frontier models, including an independent regulator that can block deployment, and she voices concern about AI escaping human control.

## Richard Blumenthal (Democrat, CT) — *flagged: runs disagreed*

Blumenthal talks about AI mainly as a source of harm that needs enforceable rules, and he often attacks Big Tech and the Trump administration for resisting them. He nods to AI's promise and calls it a new industrial revolution. His focus is on kids' safety, deepfakes, privacy, energy costs, and security threats, with some warnings about rogue models and catastrophic failure. ## Rand Paul (Republican, KY) — *flagged: runs disagreed*

Rand Paul is broadly optimistic about AI and treats it as a transformative but ordinary technology. His main worry is government use of AI for surveillance, censorship, and privacy violations, and he frames the danger as government power more than the technology itself. ## Mike Quigley (Democrat, IL) — *flagged: runs disagreed*

Quigley describes AI as having enormous potential benefits, especially in medicine, but also real and potentially deadly risks, including loss of control. He strongly favors federal regulation, including independent auditors, a kill switch, and a Select Committee, and he opposes the Trump Administration's hands-off approach. ## Elizabeth Warren (Democrat, MA) — *flagged: runs disagreed*

Elizabeth Warren frames AI mainly as a danger driven by Big Tech power: job loss, price manipulation, surveillance, higher electricity bills, and weak oversight. She repeatedly calls for strong government rules, an immediate pause on advanced AI, and taxes on AI and data centers. She also takes a hawkish line on AI chip exports to China, and she has cited the catastrophic risks raised by AI companies themselves.

## Steve Daines (Republican, MT) — *flagged: runs disagreed*

Daines talks about AI mainly as a strategic and economic race that the U.S. must win against China, and he uses race language often ('gold medalists,' 'bronze or silver is not good enough'). He says he wants light-touch 'adaptive' guardrails that don't slow innovation, is skeptical that government understands AI well enough to regulate it, and rejects moratoria. ## Warren Davidson (Republican, OH) — *flagged: runs disagreed*

Davidson treats AI as a major technology that carries both promise and risk. He puts privacy and civil liberties at the center, calling privacy 'the base layer for ethical AI' and warning about AI-enabled surveillance. He opposed the AI preemption/immunity provision in the big reconciliation bill and wants a standalone, debated bill. He also supports a federal framework and says the US should win the AI race with China without copying China.

Tuberville describes AI as an inevitable, already-present technology that America should lead, especially against China. ## Patrick Ryan (Democrat, NY) — *flagged: runs disagreed*

Patrick Ryan frames AI mainly as a threat driven by billionaire 'AI oligarchs' and a Trump administration he says is too friendly to them.
